## Honest reactions (165)

- B01-P01: The math part is real: I recomputed it and it reconciles, and it refuses inputs it does not understand, which is what I want from something that goes in a client deck. But my five proposals are a mess of base years, NNN and USF quotes, and the tool only has a flat opex line, so I would still be doing the hard normalisation myself.
- B01-P02: This is not built for my problem; it is a lease-math and ID toolkit and it says so. The parcel ID is a nice join key, but I would need the spec facts that sit on top of it before it earned a place next to CoStar.
- B01-P03: The citations are better than anything my agent produces now, and I like that it admits an owner match is an inference at 0.9. But it is DC here, not Phoenix, and it still would not tell me an LLC is a hospital's building.
- B01-P04: I came for zoning with citations and the product tells me, correctly, that it does not do zoning. The owner lookups are tidy but stop at a mailing address. Come back when there is a rules pack for Georgia and the Carolinas.
- B01-P05: The calculator I came for is correct and repeatable, and I can run it inside the client's network, which is rare. But the 38-lease job is mostly an abstraction problem and that part is a prototype with no accuracy figure.
- B01-P06: The lease math is fine but I have math. What I lack is who owes what on this building, and the product tells me up front that it does not have that. I'll watch it, not use it.
- B01-P07: It works if I lie to it and call kilowatts square feet, which tells me the engine is sound but the product was not built for me. Power data and moratorium alerts would be a different product.
- B01-P08: I'd use it every time a counter comes in because it answers 'who is winning this negotiation' with numbers I can show. It does nothing for the real problem here, which is that there are no comps, and I will not pay for more math.
- B01-P09: The provenance and rights fields are better than what my CoStar contract gives me, and the privacy page answers the 'who keeps our prompts' question. But there is nothing on loans, and a validator that lets an uncited inferred distress flag through would worry me.
- B01-P10: The numbers tie and the audit trail is the thing my analysts have been asking for. I cannot approve it as the memo engine until someone shows it reconciles to ARGUS and passes security review.
- B01-P11: It prices proposals correctly but my bottleneck is who to call. Nothing here finds a 3PL with an expiry in 24 months, so for now it is a spreadsheet replacement at best.
- B01-P12: CAM and percentage rent I would put to use right away, because I can hand the steps to a tenant who disputes the bill. The thing that actually keeps legal busy, finding the one missed exclusive, is nowhere in here.
- B01-P13: It gets effective rent right but this is a commercial leasing product wearing my problem as a costume. My legal team cares about where the competitor rent data came from, and there is none here.
- B01-P14: This is not for me, and it does not pretend otherwise: the page is about leases and parcels. I will skip it.
- B01-P15: This is the closest thing I have seen to CAM math I can defend to a tenant, and I can run it on my own box. The missing admin fee and the unproven handwriting handling mean I would still check every statement by hand for now.
- B01-P16: The leasing math is fine and I'd happily call it for proposal tie-breaks. But my four empty bays need a list of operators who are growing, and this is not that product.
- B01-P17: The idea is right and the suite normaliser is a nice start, but I found two ways it could quietly merge different suites, and I cannot mint the building IDs it depends on. I'd need both fixed and some integration before I rely on it.
- B02-P01: The math tools are exactly right and I could paste the steps into a BOV, which is more than I can say for my own spreadsheet notes. But my soft spot is cap-rate provenance and loan assumability, and FactLot says plainly it has neither, so for now it is a calculator, not a data source.
- B02-P02: The numbers are right, but I can do a rent schedule and an IRR in Sheets in two minutes. My 19-day problem is finding listings with the guarantor stated, and nothing here touches that.
- B02-P03: The calculators are clean and I would rather hand an analyst a tool-produced rent schedule than another spreadsheet. My real problem is 300 pages of amended leases, and what is here is a data format, not an abstractor.
- B02-P04: It does not force a per-SF schema on hotels, which was my first fear, and the USALI ladder checks out line by line. But without STR-type data or a way to get the seller's P&L into it, it is a nice calculator and no more.
- B02-P05: The citation discipline is better than what I get from my incumbent tools: source file, section and licence on every field. But the chain ends at a registered LLC with a name-match inference, so it does not yet do the sponsor-exposure work that costs me hours.
- B02-P06: The registry design is honest and the citations are good, but it is one small city and the search is by name. For my three states and my asset type I would still be on county assessor sites.
- B02-P07: Honest product, but it is not for site selection. The deadline tool is neat and the ID normalization is solid; neither moves a 40-acre deal.
- B02-P08: I like that facts must carry a page and a quote. But the validator waved through a 90-day ROFR against a 30-day quote, so I would not trust the badge, and the abstraction I actually need is a prototype I cannot call.
- B02-P09: The rent schedule caught the number I would have missed, and the CAM math matched to the cent. But I had to do the comparing myself, and the NDA and data-room parts of my day are untouched.
- B02-P10: The parcel key is solid and the per-field sourcing is what I want from a shared ontology. But buildings are my unit of work and there is no building ID tool, so the ontology problem is only half-answered.
- B02-P11: This is the one tool I would let my agent call instead of doing math itself, and the steps are what my reviewers want to see. It will not match lenders or normalize a T-12, so it covers the arithmetic third of my problem.
- B02-P12: The arithmetic is right, but I had to pretend kilowatts are square feet to get it. The product is built for leased space, and it never claimed otherwise.
- B02-P13: Reproducibility is real: same inputs, same bytes, formula ID and version stamped. But it is a young open-source project and the verification tool I would actually buy does not exist yet.
- B02-P14: The entity ID joined cleanly to the registry, which is what I wanted for the 1,800-tenant list. But it maps legal registrations, not brands to parents, and it does not watch anything.
- B02-P15: Free and exact, and the offline parcel ID is genuinely useful for my joins. I still have to scrape three permit portals by myself, and the collision I found makes me want to test it on real APNs first.
- B02-P16: This is the deterministic NER tool I asked for and it ties to its own published example to the cent. The data is still the problem: I can compute NER on a comp I already have, but not find one I may use.
- B02-P17: I tried to find my two problems, insurance and the post-sale tax reset, and neither exists here. The loan math is fine but I can do that in my own spreadsheet.
- B03-P01: The math is right and the steps are the kind of thing I can hand to an analyst, which is more than I can say for my in-context arithmetic. But my bottleneck is comps and rent-roll reconciliation, and this product tells me honestly that it has neither. I would wire the calculators into the bid model and nothing more.
- B03-P02: Debt sizing and IRR are exactly the places my agent drifts, and these reproduce to the penny with the formula showing. But the rest of my job (T-12 cleanup, tax reassessment, insurance, waterfall) is not here, so this is a good calculator drawer, not an underwriting platform.
- B03-P03: This is a decent calculator drawer for someone with a pro forma, but my problem is finding out whether a 90k SF climate-controlled project is real, and nothing here touches that. I would come back if it ever lists permits and agendas by county.
- B03-P04: The citation discipline is better than most vendors I see, and I like that inferred links say so. But I am here for debt and sponsors, and the product itself tells me there is no debt data, and its owner chain ends at an LLC name. Come back when mortgages are in it.
- B03-P05: The ground-lease and WALT math is correct and shows its work, but I could do that already. My hard problem is knowing who Midstate Ortho really belongs to, and this does not touch it. I would keep an eye on whether tenant entities ever appear.
- B03-P06: The math works if I lie to it about units, which tells me the product thinks in square feet. My problem is power and local politics, and the product says up front that it has no zoning data. Not for me yet.
- B03-P07: The CAM and percentage-rent tools are correct and legible, and I would use them weekly to check what my agent gets wrong. But the part that actually costs me is the lease abstraction with amendments, and what I can call today is only a validator that checks the shape of a fact, not whether the quote is real.
- B03-P08: The hotel ladder is correct and cleanly laid out, which is a pleasant surprise for a CRE product. But what sinks my deals is insurance and flood exposure, and there is nothing on either, so I would use this as a checker for my model, not as a data source.
- B03-P09: The lease math is right, but I can already do a bump schedule in a spreadsheet. The hard parts of my 45-day screen are the guarantor and the comps, and FactLot says plainly that neither is in scope.
- B03-P10: The fact format is the right shape and the honesty about ingest being a prototype is welcome. But the thing my partners reject findings over is an unverifiable pin cite, and fact_validate will happily pass a quote that is not in the lease. Build the verifier and this becomes interesting.
- B03-P11: The CAM tool is the first thing in this survey that does what my agent keeps getting wrong, and it is right to the cent under each cap reading. But the part that costs us time, comparing a returned estoppel to the lease with a citation, is not here, so it is a calculator for the dispute, not a tool for the campaign.
- B03-P12: I can see the discipline in how it cites, but a county appraisal roll is not a recorded easement, and when I ask for a Texas parcel I get a null that does not tell me whether the county is loaded. For title work I still need the instrument.
- B03-P13: The Texas rule pack is the right model: statute links, a named verifier, and a 'not covered' list. But it is a property-tax deadline, not zoning, so I have nothing to hand a lender. If zoning packs arrived in this form I would pay attention.
- B03-P14: Debt sizing is the one piece of my workflow where this is a clear upgrade over in-context arithmetic: five runs matched my own to the dollar and the binding constraint is explicit. The fraud tie-out, rent control and insurance work are untouched, so it is a calculator I would adopt, not a credit platform.
- B03-P15: The rent-schedule check is correct and the terms are refreshingly plain, but I still have to type every lease term in myself, which is the part that takes my time. If the lease reader ever leaves prototype and works on phone scans, I would try it the same day.
- B03-P16: The fact format is the right idea and it can say 'do not redistribute', which I care about. But there is no signature and the validator lets through anything shaped correctly, so I could not tell a buyer 'verify this' yet.
- B03-P17: The registry does what it says and cites well, and I was able to find yard-like parcels from land versus building area. But my question is whether truck parking is permitted and whether the site is on a contaminated-sites list, and neither is anywhere in the data.
- B04-P01: The lease math is clean, it reproduced to the cent and showed me its discounting convention, which is more than my own pandas rebuild does. But my hold/sell fails on missing comps and an ARGUS-like cash-flow engine, and FactLot does not pretend otherwise; it is a helpful calculator, not a workflow I can lean on.
- B04-P02: The cap math is what I wanted: three cap conventions, each one matched my recompute and showed its steps, so the human can stop redoing it in Excel. But the hard part of my audit is getting reliable inputs out of a 140-page scanned lease and a landlord PDF, and FactLot only helps after that step.
- B04-P03: The loan-sizing and IRR math is correct and shows its steps, but I can already do that in code; my memo has holes where market inputs go, and FactLot has none of those. Honest and tidy about its scope, just not what I need yet.
- B04-P04: The calculator does what it says and is free, which beats my inherited Excel template. But the one thing that burned me, the mid-year tenant, is still on me: it will happily compute the wrong answer from a prorated share and say nothing.
- B04-P05: The way it shows its evidence, source file, record number, method and confidence, is the way I would want a parent link presented to a board. But it stops at 'who owns the parcel', not 'who is the parent of this PLLC', so my credit watchlist is no better today.
- B04-P06: The pieces are the right shape, a cited fact with page and quote, a content hash for the amendment, a trustworthy rent schedule, but I have to assemble every estoppel step myself and the lease reading part is only a prototype. I would watch this, not rely on it.
- B04-P07: The ID join helps and the citations are what I want, but I could not retrieve a single Brooklyn fact, and the pricing page itself says the hosted registry is not open. I would check back when NYC is live; today it is a calculator I do not need.
- B04-P08: The ID rules are sensible and the provenance fields are what I would require, but the hard part of my acquisition, mapping three seller formats and finding missing amendments, is not offered. As a join-key library it is useful; as an onboarding tool it is not there.
- B04-P09: I tried to find something to use and the closest thing was existing parcel areas for a couple of DC buildings. That is not my problem. It is honest about not covering permits, so no hard feelings, but it is not for me.
- B04-P10: This is the part I can use: the liability, ROU and cost recomputed to the cent and the tool even warned about the rate. The cents that do not foot in the schedule are small, but small differences are what my auditors comment on, so I would fix that first.
- B04-P11: The ladder is right to the penny and shows its steps, which beats re-deriving RevPAR in context. But the work is mapping 28 operators' budget packages into those inputs and benchmarking labor and insurance, and none of that is here. A helpful ruler, not the workshop.
- B04-P12: It did the schedules and the PV right, and the citation style is what arbitration needs. But a reset fight is about land value and zoning, and FactLot has neither; I would use it only for the rent arithmetic.
- B04-P13: I could force a per-kW lease comparison out of a per-SF tool and the arithmetic held, but that is a calculator trick, not data. Where my problem lives, power availability, FactLot has nothing, as I expected from a general CRE provider.
- B04-P14: The fact format can hold exactly what my attorney wants, quote, page, document hash. But the format will accept a 'fact' without the quote, and the extraction and conflict-check pieces are not here, so I cannot get from a proposed tenant to a list of conflicting clauses.
- B04-P15: The normalization maths is right and free, and I can use it to compare a quote against my own suite. But my actual problem is not the maths, it is getting the competing rents and opex in the first place, and FactLot does not touch that.
- B04-P16: The schema design is closer to what I need than anything else I could try, per-field page, quote, confidence and verifier, and it is open source, which is a hard requirement. But it is a format, not a QA tool: no crosswalks, no exporters, and the validator does not even check that the cited document ID is real.
- B04-P17: The defeasance and the Texas deadline came out right and cited, which I can use. But my model was always fine; the guesses are in the inputs, and FactLot gives me no market inputs, loan outcomes or tenant credit. It is useful at the edges of the decision, not the middle of it.
- B05-P01: This is built for people doing lease and loan math, not for a maintenance supervisor racing a heat-emergency SLA. The cited-fact format is the right idea for my COI problem, but there is no extractor and nothing that touches Yardi, so my supervisor would still be re-keying.
- B05-P02: The recon arithmetic is exactly what I want to stop doing in a spreadsheet, and it reproduced to the cent. It does not solve my real problem, which is knowing which amendment controls and being able to cite the clause to the tenant's auditor. I would use it as the engine behind my own recon template, not as the system of record.
- B05-P03: The CAM check is useful and I like that it shows its work, so the owner can see it. But my real time sink is chasing delinquent tenants and comparing sealcoat quotes, and there is nothing here for either. I would try it for true-ups and leave it at that.
- B05-P04: The building blocks (document hashes, suite IDs, a fact with page and quote) are exactly what I would build a claim file on. But today it only gives me the plumbing, not the surrender list, and the quote check is described but not something I could run.
- B05-P05: The NER math is right and I can hand the steps to compliance. What I cannot get is the comps, which is the thing that actually costs me six weeks. I would use it for the arithmetic every renewal, but it does not replace the appraiser.
- B05-P06: This is the right way to do legal timelines: cite the statute, say who checked it and say what is not covered. But it only covers Texas property tax, so for my lien sales I am still building the calendar by hand.
- B05-P07: The way each fact shows its source and date is the habit I want from a data layer. But it gives me one assessor number from DC, nothing for Seattle, and the area figures I scanned include some that look off. For BEPS the registry is not yet a source I could file with.
- B05-P08: The hotel math checks out and reads like my M3 ladder, which is nice but not what keeps me up at night. The renewal problem is wrong roof ages and building facts carriers will accept, and the registry gives me area, year built and an assessed value only, for DC.
- B05-P09: The licensing and self-hosting posture is right, and the fact schema is flexible enough to hold my power terms. But there is no extractor I can run and no proration math, so today it is a schema without the engine I need.
- B05-P10: The comparison is a clean way to put three spaces on one footing and it checked out. My tenants ignore ranges because I cannot say what the existing space holds, and this has nothing on suite conditions.
- B05-P11: The finance primitives are exact and that stops my model drifting on escalation. But I have 120 PCAs to normalize and no calculator or extractor here for that, so I would use it as a calculator sidecar only.
- B05-P12: The ID join is real and it works the way I would want it to, offline and then in the registry. But the answer to 'can I build 400k SF here' is zoning and utilities, which this does not touch, and my counties are not loaded.
- B05-P13: It is a lease-and-loan toolbox. I read the materials looking for anything about base-building rules or DOB and found none, and the tests found none either.
- B05-P14: The fact format is the right shape for evidence, but all it enforces is structure. It accepts a human-verified claim with no named verifier and a source that is just an email subject, so it would not stand up as my audit trail.
- B05-P15: The ID join story is relevant, and cited owners are useful. But my filter is floor-to-floor and live load, not year built, and there is no way to search by attribute here, so I would still be calling brokers.
- B05-P16: I can get the amortization number and run it through CAM, and I like that every formula is visible. But I am stitching it together from a loan sizer and a CAM tool, and the clause interpretation is still on me.
- B05-P17: I can make it add numbers exactly, which is nice. But that is a long way from reviewing a draw, and nothing here parses a G703 or tracks waivers.
- B06-P01: The sizing math is clean and I could show the steps to model validation, but that is the easy part of my annual review. The expensive part is the scanned PDFs and the licensed market data, and FactLot says plainly it does not do either. I would test it on one file and keep Excel as the system of record.
- B06-P02: The sizing tool does what my Excel does and shows its work, which is nice but not why I would change anything. The ownership lookup is the interesting piece and it worked on DC, but it gave me 27 condo-unit rows for one sponsor and no debt data. Come back when the Sun Belt counties are hosted and rolled up by building.
- B06-P03: The provenance model is the right shape and I like that bad facts get rejected. But the one thing I would pay for, resolving 'DG #14523' to a parent, simply is not there, and the product says so. I would keep an eye on it and not spend a day on it.
- B06-P04: The loan sizing is fine and free, but I can do it in my template already. The reason I looked was the tax projection and the retrofit and rent-control layers, and none of that exists for California. The honest Texas-only note saved me time.
- B06-P05: I came for the mapping problem and the product tells me, correctly, that it does not do it. The prepayment calculator matched my independent recomputation to the cent, but that is arithmetic I could also do in Excel. No change from today.
- B06-P06: This does the DSCR and stress math without a minimum fee, and I checked its numbers against my own. It does not give me the data I cannot afford, but the math part I can use next week.
- B06-P07: The calculators are tidy and honest, but my first question was power, and the product has no answer and does not pretend to. I would not integrate it.
- B06-P08: NER math is where my agent loses its way and this tool fixed that part. The tool shows its convention, which is good, but I still need leases and comps, and neither is in the box. Useful as an inner function, not a product I would build a workflow around.
- B06-P09: The fact schema is exactly what a verified comp should look like, and I could contribute mine to it. But there is no Kansas data and no sales data, so today it saves me no phone calls. I would look again if a county register of deeds feed appeared.
- B06-P10: I like the NER math and the rights flag more than I expected from a free tool, and both numbers checked out. But it ranks what I give it; it does not give me comps, and its landlord view treats my gross comp as if the owner paid no expenses. My legal team would approve the licence model, not the coverage.
- B06-P11: The provenance on each field is the best part and exactly what my lender clients ask for. But my merges break at the building level, and the product gives me 24 condo APNs with no building above them. The materials promise building IDs, so I would ask the founder what that means before building on it.
- B06-P12: Everything I do is a check against the recorded deed, and FactLot has no deeds. The rent tie-out math was right, but I can do that on a spreadsheet. Thin on the only thing I would pay for.
- B06-P13: The Texas deadline tool is precise and I checked six dates against my own count; I would use it as a calendar sanity check each spring. It does not reduce the real work, which is equity comps and Cook County, and the product states that Cook County is not available.
- B06-P14: This does the arithmetic I stopped trusting an LLM with and shows each line. It does nothing for the data side of my work, which is where I pay STR. I would use it for the ladder and keep Excel for the department detail.
- B06-P15: Determinism and version stamps are what my auditors ask for and the repeat calls came back identical. But the hard part, getting terms out of signed PDFs with page cites, is a prototype with no accuracy number, so the product does not yet touch my quarterly process.
- B06-P16: The schema looks like it was designed by someone who understands that history must not be overwritten, which counts for a lot with me. But I could not retrieve a source document or ask for a past date, and there is no zoning data. I would watch the project and not rely on it in a deposition.
- B07-P01: It is honest that it cannot answer my Frisco question, and the calculators are clean, but that is not the job I need done. I would check back when a Texas suburb is actually queryable and zoning has citations.
- B07-P02: The suite-ID and provenance ideas are exactly the right shape for my board-citation problem, but the data that would make them useful is not here and address matching is too brittle to trust for provider dedup. I would watch it, not buy it.
- B07-P03: The comparison maths is good and free, which beats my Excel templates for LOI side-by-sides. But my real problem is stale listings and invisible exclusives, and FactLot has nothing there yet.
- B07-P04: Good that it names rural states, bad that I cannot query any of them. Until I can screen 1.5-2.5 acre parcels with zoning and sewer unknowns flagged, it is a curiosity.
- B07-P05: The design philosophy is right, but I cannot call the thing I need. A validator that accepts a wrong value next to its quote does not give counsel comfort.
- B07-P06: The renewal NPV is useful and checks out. But the question I get asked is whether the mall owner is distressed, and FactLot says plainly it has no debt data.
- B07-P07: The vocabulary is there, the data is not. If someone fills building.power_amps with a source and a date, I am interested.
- B07-P08: It does not touch the problem that eats my analysts' day. The IDs are tidy, but a legal-entity registry is not a brand map.
- B07-P09: The math layer is exactly what I want finance to stop rebuilding, and it reproduced to the cent. But the inputs still come from my own re-reading of PDFs, and without SOC 2 I cannot even pilot it.
- B07-P10: The calculator is solid and I will keep it. But my power in the negotiation comes from comps and the landlord's refinance clock, and FactLot has neither.
- B07-P11: The math is auditable in the way my auditors would like and it reproduces independently. What it does not do is read the amendment or tie each number to a page, which is the part my team actually spends time on.
- B07-P12: Deterministic, open and honest about its limits, which model risk will like. It is a calculator kit, though, and the clause facts that decide my 90 closures are not in it.
- B07-P13: This replaces my Excel template for the maths, and I could audit the PV. But it ranked on rentable SF, which would have steered a client wrongly if I had not checked, and it cannot model base years.
- B07-P14: The NPV pieces are fine and free but I could do them in a spreadsheet. The risky parts of my sublease are specs, landlord distress and subtenant credit, and none exist here.
- B07-P15: Honest and clean, but it is an office-and-retail tool wearing a generic coat. The kW workaround proves the maths is fine, not that it understands data centers.
- B07-P16: The ID design is what I wanted and the owner-link evidence is the right style. But my five spellings of '100 N Main' would mostly still fail, and no building IDs exist.
- B08-P01: I like that it tells me up front it has no zoning, because zoning is the whole reason I would pay. The calculators are correct but I already have Excel; what I need is the rules, and the rules pack that exists covers a Texas tax deadline, not my envelope.
- B08-P02: This is a different product from what I need. The math tools are fine and honest, but my hard problem is AB 98 and PA moratoria, and there is nothing here for either. I would check back if an entitlement pack ever shows up.
- B08-P03: The philosophy fits us: open, inspectable, correctable. But the product today is the container without the Seattle zoning content. I will watch the repo rather than depend on it.
- B08-P04: Not for us today. The no-logging promise is the one thing that caught my eye, but I would need an audit, and there is no power data to be confidential about.
- B08-P05: The citation model is the best thing I saw: file, section, licence, confidence. But it is DC only and it stops at the LLC, which is exactly where my work begins. Interesting design, not my tool yet.
- B08-P06: The loan-sizing output is clean and I could paste it. It does nothing for my real problem, which is statute eligibility per parcel. I would only come back if California law shows up as rules.
- B08-P07: Correct math, but Excel does that and my hard problem is the pipeline in a dozen small cities, which this does not touch. I would not put it next to Radius+.
- B08-P08: The provenance model is the right shape for a workfile - it is the first tool I have seen that puts the AI-use right on the fact. But there is no NYC and no zoning, so for now it is a design I admire.
- B08-P09: The deadline tool is the kind of small, correct, cited thing an agent should never improvise, and I would let it run. It is a thin slice of my job though; the engine and the CAD data are where the money is.
- B08-P10: A wrong deadline is my career-ending event, so I respect that it only offers the one rule it verified. But that means it solves one of my 35 states. Come back with the rest and a security report.
- B08-P11: The citation schema is what I would design, and the verbatim-quote rule for Ingest is promising. But the extractor is a prototype and the tools do not read my PDFs, so today it only stores what I already extracted.
- B08-P12: The P&L ladder is correct and useful as the starting point of the argument. The hard part - taking out business value and arguing the cap rate - is not there, so it saves me an hour, not a petition.
- B08-P13: The recovery side is solid and the warning about the order of cap and base year is exactly what an auditor would ask. The forecast side - the levy and cap rules - is the missing half.
- B08-P14: This is built for lease and loan math and parcel IDs, not for my asset register. I like the offline mode, and I note an inferred fact can pass without a source, which I would not accept in our file.
- B08-P15: For the arithmetic part this is the cleanest thing I tried: I recomputed everything and it holds, and the formula is shown. It cannot give me comps, though, and comps are what opposing counsel attacks.
- B08-P16: I wanted comps and a form, and FactLot says plainly it has no sales. At least it does not pretend. If Ohio sales ever show up, cheap per-call is exactly my model.
- B09-P01: It is honest about being parcel-and-owner data, and the citations are the right shape, but my job is building-level COPE on 400-row SOVs and this does not touch it. I would not put it in front of a cat-model vendor yet.
- B09-P02: The sizing engine is what I would want in a credit memo: every step is there and it reproduced exactly. But the part of my hardest task that hurts, premium benchmarks and millage, is simply not in it, and I would still be building the shock by hand.
- B09-P03: It's free and the citations are nice, but what I need is stories, roof and sprinklers, and all I get is one area number and a year. I'd still be guessing from aerials and the carrier would still be rejecting on inspection.
- B09-P04: The schema is what I would have designed myself, which is encouraging. But the validator is lenient and nothing is stored or attested, so I would still be assembling the evidence trail manually for the auditors.
- B09-P05: The IDs and the citation format are tidy, but I lose a week re-keying carrier forms and this does not touch permits or submissions. I would keep an eye on it, not open an account.
- B09-P06: I said the real gap is identity resolution, and this is not it: it resolves parcels, one call at a time, with no SLA. I would not put it in a vendor-risk review for production use.
- B09-P07: At least it told me honestly it has no zoning data. The one rule it does have is done right, but I need the district, the SUP and the parking formula with code sections, and none of that is here.
- B09-P08: The audit-trail idea is what my JV partners want, but the one calculator I asked for is not there, and the GFA I got is a single parcel number. I would watch the calculator list, not sign up.

- B09-P10: The data model fits what the verifier asks for, and hashing the bills is the right instinct. But there is no BERDO content, no packet and no signature, so I still end up with the 300-file folder.
- B09-P11: It does the first half of my 30-minute lookup and shows me how sure it is, which I like. But I still have to find a person and I cannot call Denver data yet, so my cold-email list would not get any shorter.

- B09-P13: The math checks out and it's free, but it solves a problem I do not have. For my Seattle and LA filings it has nothing, and it didn't look like the team knows hotel brand data plumbing.
- B09-P14: The sizing tool is solid and I can put its steps in an IC memo. But my reason to quote fast on collateral is BPS exposure and debt maturity, and there is none of either, so the hard part of my screen is still manual.
- B09-P15: I get the owner corporation but not the person who signs, and no LL97 number to put in a proposal. The NPV and IRR tools are fine but I already have my own proposal model. Not worth a switch yet.
- B09-P16: It's honest, but the unit is the parcel, and my problem is several buildings on one parcel with different uses. There is no CBPS content at all. I would not spend time wiring it up.
- B10-P01: The math checks out and it is free, so I would let my CPA's agent use it for debt sizing and IRR per candidate. But it does not touch the hard part of my 45 days, which is finding live NNN deals, knowing the guarantor and getting comps; for that this is a calculator, not a deal-flow tool.
- B10-P02: For refinance math I would use it. For my real fire, the Board of Revision appeals, there is nothing here for Ohio, and I could not even tell from the site whether Columbus is loaded. I would check back if a county list appears.
- B10-P03: Good for the arithmetic my chat model keeps flubbing, but my real problem is knowing how much storage is coming within five miles of Lawton, and this does not touch it. I would use the calculators and keep paying for what I pay for now.

- B10-P05: This fixes the exact bug I had: it will not apply a cap the wrong way, and I can show the tenant the steps. It does not read my leases or the association PDFs, and it has no admin-fee line, so I would still be doing the data entry.
- B10-P06: The provenance model is the right shape for my compliance file, and the document hash is a genuine audit-trail primitive. But the checker I called would pass a fact that contradicts its own quote, and nothing reads a 200-page PPM, so today it is bookkeeping, not a time saver.
- B10-P07: This does the one thing my agent kept getting inconsistent, and it shows its work so my banker can follow it. It will not tell me who lends on small mixed-use or what Cook County will do to my taxes, which are the two things I actually lose sleep over.

- B10-P10: The suite ID idea would help dedupe, and the proposal math is right, but my day is spent re-keying the same suite into four systems and nothing here pushes data anywhere. It is adjacent to my job, not in it.
